The Notre Dame Global Adaptation Initiative Country Index summarizes a country's vulnerability to climate change and other global challenges in combination with its readiness to improve resilience.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.30/02/Add.03

Page 124

- development and planning of inland areas shall consider the need for integrated management of water resources bearing in mind the potential impacts these may have on coastal areas'; iv) Timor-Leste Initial National Communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (2014), which identifies some key adaptation actions relevant to EWS; v) Intended Nationally Determined Contributions (INDC) Timor-Leste (2016) - The project will directly address the priority measures for adaptation to natural disasters identified in the INDC; and vi) United Nations Sustainable Development Cooperation Framework Timor-Leste (20212025) - The project is fully aligned with outcome 6, 'sustainable management of natural resources and resilience to climate change', which identifies 'strengthening early warning system and capacities' as a specific priority.

## 3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: N/A

41. The proposal demonstrates good return on investment. The proposed interventions have been demonstrated to significantly reduce operations and maintenance compared to traditional solutions due to cost-effective technologies and approaches. The business delivery model to be developed will enable effective coverage of operations and maintenance, sustainability and replication.
42. The proposal's cost-benefit analysis (CBA) for the impact-based MHEWS in Timor-Leste shows a positive net present value (NPV) of USD 77.9 million at a 5% discount rate. The economic internal rate of return (EIRR) of 104.5% exceeds the discount rate, demonstrating the viability of the intervention. A sensitivity analysis was conducted to test the robustness of this assessment based on decreases and increases (of key parameters) in benefits by 10%. Although the EIRR decreased with those simulated cost benefit changes, the EIRR remained well above the 10% threshold in both cases (90.4% and 91.5% respectively). Additionally, although more pessimistic scenarios, combining a decrease in benefits by 10% and an increase in costs by 10%, the results show very high NPV and EIRR (US $41.2 million and 79.5%). In conclusion, the proposed impact-based MHEWS intervention demonstrates significant benefits.
43. The level of co-financing is relatively low (3.4% of total financing). However, the project aims to develop and establish a sustainable business model under sub-Activity 1.1.5 which will contribute to leverage financing from the Government and users for the sustainability of climate information services.

## IV. Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

## 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards

44. The project is expected to contribute to upgrading Timor-Leste's EWS, thereby improving generation and use of climate information for climate risk management and enhancing the climate resilience of communities to weather-related disasters. The project focuses on strengthening the policy, legal and institutional frameworks for climate information and MHEWS. In addition, the project will modernize the national meteorological service by installing new and upgraded observation equipment. The AE has classified the proposal as a low-risk project, which is confirmed as being equivalent to GCF's category C classification by the Secretariat and within the AE's accreditation level.
45. A number of interventions under the project include capacity-building for DNMG, the national meteorological service, by strengthening observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts and dissemination and communication of information. Equipment that will be provided by the project to upgrade and modernize the meteorological observation system will be installed, operated and maintained on existing government-owned

<!-- image -->

- land, thereby reducing costs and, importantly, avoiding social impacts associated with land acquisition or physical displacement. Potential minimal risks are mainly environmental, spatially and temporally restricted, and reversible. Examples may include vegetation clearing, sediment movement, disturbance of riverine and riparian ecosystems, and occupational safety risks to workers. The environmental and social safeguards report describes mitigation measures for some of the potential minimal risks such as the use of personal protective equipment for technicians installing hydrometeorological apparatus.
46. The AE considers that, as all Timorese are indigenous, special measures such as an indigenous peoples plan are not necessary to safeguard their interests and ensure they benefit fully. Nevertheless, due to the variety of indigenous peoples in Timor-Leste and the prevalence of extreme poverty, careful attention is needed to ensure the project is socially inclusive and responsive to the needs of all persons in Timor-Leste, including all indigenous peoples. Importantly, the project includes targeted interventions based on the differential needs of specific vulnerable groups to ensure that all persons in Timor-Leste are equally protected from adverse impacts and can fully benefit from the project. The feasibility study includes preliminary beneficiary selection criteria, which will be further elaborated and refined during the inception phase of the project. A beneficiary feedback mechanism will be established through the project steering committee to monitor that the selection criteria continue to be appropriate throughout project implementation and that the most vulnerable people are being reached.
47. The project will engage a specialist with an expertise in environmental and social safeguards to ensure the continuous screening of activities for risks and the implementation of mitigation measures for minimal environmental and social impacts in coordination with contractors and the EE, the Secretary of State for the Environment (SSE).
48. Stakeholder consultations were undertaken during the project development stage in a series of meetings and workshops. The consultations and stakeholder engagement plan includes a report on consultations held with national authorities and non-governmental organizations, including details regarding the dates and venues and issues discussed at various meetings. Furthermore, the project has developed a stakeholder engagement plan for the implementation stage. The plan identifies stakeholders, including community groups, that will be engaged. The responsibilities of managing and implementing the plan will rest with the project manager, who will be recruited as part of the project management unit (PMU). The project manager will also work closely with the EE in the implementation of the stakeholder engagement plan. During the inception phase of the project and prior to commencing implementation, elements of the engagement strategy will be refined-the AE is requested to ensure that the stakeholder engagement plan is revised accordingly at that time.
49. The project will use the AE's stakeholder response mechanism to receive and coordinate the resolution of grievances received. In addition, the GCF Independent Redress Mechanism will be available to affected or potentially affected people and communities. In line with the GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy, the GCF indigenous peoples focal point will be available for assistance at any stage, including before a claim has been made. Details of how both mechanisms can be contacted are presented in the environmental and social safeguards report. It is recommended that the AE ensures that information on both mechanisms is communicated to stakeholders prior to commencing implementation.

## 4.2 Gender policy

50. The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the Gender Policy of GCF.

<!-- image -->

51. The gender assessment discusses the enabling environment for the pursuit of gender equity and women's empowerment in the context of the project in Timor-Leste. The constitution of Timor-Leste protects against discrimination based on gender and calls for equality between men and women in familial, political, economic, social and cultural life. TimorLeste has ratified the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women and adopted the Sustainable Development Goals, which include a commitment to achieve gender equality and the empowerment of all women and girls. It also launched a National Action Plan for United Nations Security Council Resolution 1325 on Women, Peace and Security and the Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction. The pursuit of gender equality is supported by the Secretary of State for Equality and Inclusion that works with gender working groups to ensure that gender priorities are mainstreamed into government activities. The Timor-Leste Strategic Development Plan also includes gender aspects across policies, laws, processes, livelihood support, employment initiatives, education and leadership, reproductive health and the reduction of gender-based violence (GBV).
52. The gender assessment was completed based on a desk review, primary data collection and stakeholder consultation at various levels including women and other vulnerable groups. Despite a recently strengthened enabling environment, there continues to be significant barriers facing women. There is very limited access to education for young girls and women as well as limited access to maternal and childcare services. Malnutrition, food insecurity and hunger in Timor-Leste are among the highest in the world. Traditional societal and cultural norms assign strict gender roles for men and women, which often lead to discriminatory practices against women. At the local level, women have been largely excluded from decisionmaking roles or roles that can influence decisions. However, women have recently gained inroads in parliamentary representation with 25 of 68 seats being held by women. Women's access to land is very limited with 80 per cent of land being acquired through customary rights, which mostly exclude women. The lack of land rights among women perpetuates their inability to access financial security, independence and ability to improve their livelihoods. Violence against women is widespread in Timor-Leste with almost 40 per cent of women having experienced physical violence during their adult life. Less than 1 in 4 seek help and only 4 per cent report to police mainly because of fear of repercussions, family pressures, economic dependence and self-blame.
53. The project priority sectors include agriculture and food security, disaster risk reduction, energy, health and water. The gender assessment has identified key barriers that need to be addressed through the project. In the agricultural sector, there continues to be gender gaps in productivity attributed to lower access to factors of production including hired labor, farming tools, limited involvement in cash crop production and farmers groups. These are due to lower literacy rates, low access to land and finance, women's traditional roles and household duties, time poverty and cultural norms. Lower income levels among women also have a compounding effect on food security. Women's vulnerability to disasters is compounded by limited access and input in decision-making related to risk reduction, preparation and response. This is compounded by their generally lower access and control of resources, information sources and networks, and fewer assets to support resiliency after climate change shocks including droughts and floods. Women play a key role in collecting wood and biomass, which account for 90 per cent of the energy needs of Timor-Leste. This contributes to the time burden and time poverty that afflict many women. Kerosene lamps and candles provide light for the majority of the population and have significant health impacts on women and girls due to their domestic duties. The high prices and fluctuation of kerosene prices place a significant burden on the poorest, many of whom are women. Women's domestic duties related to water and sanitation increase their exposure to health risks and also contribute to time poverty. The gender assessment also identifies key gender issues in climate and early warning services. These include: men's primary positions in decision-making and information transmission; women's less access to communication technologies; sociocultural norms in decision-making,

<!-- image -->

- land and resource tenure that disadvantage women in their ability to invest in risk reduction actions and investments; information dissemination that often does not account for genderdifferentiated roles and information needs; and women's lower literacy and language barriers that may limit their access to important information.
54. The AE has provided a gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirement of the Gender Policy of GCF. The gender action plan provides activities that address the challenges faced by women and includes baseline, indicators, targets, timelines and budgets. The project will engage a gender expert to ensure gender is integrated throughout the project, including gender expertise in decision-making bodies. Proposed activities are aimed at mainstreaming gender into institutional and policy frameworks, legislation and delivery models for climate services. This includes the active participation of women and vulnerable groups in consultations and the inclusion of their priorities in the formulation and execution of these activities. Mainstreaming also involves actively addressing barriers and gender gaps that impact women's participation and engagement, such as the gender-specific needs of climate-vulnerable sectors-with a particular focus on subsistence agriculture and other sectors where women are key players and where their actions have impacts on household food, nutritional and water security. The gender action plan also indicates activities to mainstream gender in the strengthening of observations, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts. Specific activities include ensuring gender equity in human resource development, training and capacity-building; establishing climate services for health; and ensuring meaningful participation and engagement of women in decision-making processes. The third result area in the gender action plan is designed to mainstream gender into improved dissemination and communication of risk information and EWS. Specific activities include measures to ensure that EWS are tailored to the specific needs and capabilities of women and other vulnerable and/or marginalized groups and that gender equality and gender mainstreaming best practices are incorporated into the EWS standard operating procedures. The gender action plan also includes activities and measures to mainstream gender into enhanced climate risk management capacity. These include ensuring gender equality in capacity development, analysing gender-specific and other intersectional vulnerabilities, and ensuring that the community action plans include actions that are responsive to the specific needs of women and other vulnerable and/or marginalized groups. Since GBV has been identified as a critical challenge, the project will take measures to minimize the risk of increased GBV and is developing measures to ensure that women have equal access to the grievance mechanism that will offer a clear process for all female beneficiaries providing comments and raising grievances.

## 4.3 Risks

## 4.3.1. Overall project assessment (medium risk)

55. GCF is requested to provide a grant of USD 20.98 million to finance climate information services and MHEWS in Timor-Leste. The Government of Timor-Leste is providing USD 0.75 million in in-kind contribution. There is no co-financing from the AE.
56. Timor-Leste is an LDC and SIDS. There is limited scope for the government to incur public debt and the GCF investment will not generate direct revenue during the project implementation period. Thus, the government is seeking a full grant from GCF. The project includes several interventions that will support private sector engagement and the identification of long-term sources of funds and capitalization opportunities. While the project is expected to arouse the interest of the private sector in utilizing climate information and EWS, cost recovery and revenue generation are not considered feasible within five years of the project implementation period.

<!-- image -->

## 4.3.2. Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current project (medium-high risk)

57. The AE, UNEP, has a track record in implementing projects related to climate change information management and EWS in SIDS. The AE will act as a co-EE for certain sub-activities under components 1, 2 and 4.
58. SSE is another co-EE. It is noted that SSE is also the national designated authority. It has experience in implementing projects financed by external donors; however, most projects over the past five years are micro scale (less than USD 1.3 million). The EE's capacity assessment to be carried out by the AE is pending. The AE may need to support the EE during the implementation and strengthen the capacity of the EE.

## 4.3.3. Project-specific execution risks (medium risk)

59. Government approval: The policy frameworks and legislations to be developed under activity 1.1 will require the approval of the government, which is beyond the project scope. For example, the draft of the Meteorological Act (sub activity 1.1.2) will require the approval of the Council of Ministers. The climate data strategy and action plan for climate data management and governance will also be submitted for approval at the government level (sub activity 1.1.3). The approval will be an important step as it will formalize the role of DNMG and provide it with budget security.
60. O&amp;M risk: The funding proposal identified O&amp;M related constraints as risks with high probabilities. The country may face some challenges to maintain highly technical equipment due to the limited financing and human resources. The project will hire new technical staff with necessary qualifications to maintain the equipment. If staff with appropriate qualifications are not locally available, an international technician will need to train the staff. DNMG has provided a letter indicating it would take responsibility for securing O&amp;M after project implementation for up to 20 years. However, a detailed financing plan is not available. A financial framework for sustainable operation (sub-activity 1.1.6) will be developed during the project.
61. Economic analysis: The AE provided a cost-benefit analysis over a 10-year horizon considering avoided economic damages and improved productivity in key sectors as benefits. The analysis resulted in the EIRR of 104.5 per cent in 10 years, considering benefits for avoided economic damages and losses of infrastructure, physical assets and crops. A sensitivity analysis still shows positive NPVs even if benefits fall by 10 per cent or costs increase by 10 per cent.

## 4.3.4. GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

62. In the case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is not material.

## 4.3.5. Compliance risk (high risk)

63. The Office of Risk Management and Compliance has requested the AE to submit the final risk assessment of the counterparties on several occasions but, due to country-specific factors, the AE could not produce the same. Since ORMC/Compliance cannot finalize its assessment of the project without this crucial piece, it has determined a high risk rating. The Secretariat wishes to remind the AE of its obligations under clause 4.05 (b) of the accreditation master agreement (AMA), and recommends allowing sufficient time in advance to make the necessary arrangements so that all applicable provisions of the AMA can be met before the funding proposal is submitted for technical review.

## 4.4 Fiduciary

65. UNEP will be the AE for the project. SSE in Timor-Leste will serve as the national EE. SSE will be accountable to UNEP for project execution at the national level and for the effective and efficient use of resources. At the request of the national designated authority, UNEP will undertake limited EE functions.
66. As the AE, UNEP will be responsible for managing the implementation, financial management, evaluation, reporting and closure of the activities under the project. UNEP will monitor and supervise the execution of the project and ensure the proper management and application of GCF grant proceeds by the EEs. UNEP will ensure that the grant proceeds are utilized in accordance with the terms of the funded activity agreement to be entered into between GCF and UNEP and the AMA. UNEP will also assume a limited role as EE.
67. UNEP will set up and manage a PMU, drawing on its Global Support Services Agreement with the United Nations Office for Project Services, where necessary. The PMU will provide management and support to the national implementation of the project-through the coordination of the national EE (SSE) and technical partners involved in project implementation, in line with their obligations under the respective legal instruments-and will coordinate to ensure that reports are received. The PMU will consolidate all semiannual progress reports and quarterly financial reports, including co-financing reports and annual audit reports, from the EE and technical partners and submit these to the AE. The PMU will provide guidance and source expertise as needed on project management, financial management, procurement and technical issues. It will establish contact with other development partners working with Timor-Leste to ensure that activities in related fields are complementary and to seek opportunities for collaboration. The PMU will also provide secretariat services to the project steering committee.
68. UNEP will enter into an appropriate agreement (Project Cooperation Agreement or PCA) with SSE for the execution of the project. The PCA will establish clear roles and responsibilities for the delivery of the proposed activities; the schedule and conditions for instalments; the determination of the prevailing fiduciary standards; and the terms and conditions for arbitrations and termination of contract. The PCA will include specific obligations for the national EE on project execution, financial management, personnel administration and reporting, as well as arbitration and liability terms. The national EE will be responsible for establishing national project implementation in a relevant part of the government administration to provide implementation guidance and support to national service providers and technical partners (see below section). The national EE will thus provide technical and implementation guidance and will facilitate cooperation and coordination among the national service providers. It will be accountable to and submit regular progress reports to the PMU.
69. The financial management and procurement within the project will be guided by United Nations financial regulations, rules and practices, as well as UNEP's project manual. The

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.30/02/Add.03

Page 130

- financial rules of UNEP, which follow International Public Sector Accounting Standards (IPSAS), are promulgated pursuant to the Financial Regulations and Rules of the United Nations. Within this context, funding allocation mechanisms are managed in accordance with United Nations rules and procedures, including eligibility criteria, proposal evaluation processes, quality assurance and control, project monitoring and supervision. UNEP is audited annually by the United Nations Board of Auditors and has established dedicated trust funds for GCF resources. SSE, as the national EE, follows the Government of Timor-Leste's financial and procurement rules. During the project's inception phase, UNEP will conduct more detailed assessments of SSE's capacity to undertake procurement in line with United Nations regulations, rules and processes. This assessment will guide the procurement monitoring plan that will be agreed between UNEP and SSE. The same will be carried out for the technical partners supporting execution in Timor-Leste (FAO, RIMES, IFRC, BMKG and ICTP), who are subject to financial and procurement guidelines acceptable to their members' or governments' financial and procurement policies and guidelines and are audited annually.

## 4.5 Results monitoring and reporting

70. As an adaptation initiative, the project aims to benefit about 1,293,119 beneficiaries including 1,034,495 direct beneficiaries. The intervention is expected to deliver direct benefits to 80% of the population of Timor-Leste, of which women comprise 511,690 direct and 639,613 indirect beneficiaries as per the gender-disaggregated metrics for the GCF core indicator for adaptation.
71. The project targets different types of adaptation activities which are expected to generate impact across two adaptation result areas A1 - Most vulnerable people, communities and regions and A2 - Health and well-being, food and water security. This is properly demonstrated in the Theory of Change diagram which clearly outlines how change is understood to come about in the country. The goal statement is properly articulated, and stated outcomes are defined in a manner that is adequately supportive of meeting the ultimate project goal.
72. The logical framework appropriately applies GCF Fund level (impact and outcome) RMF/PMF indicators, and the project has built in baselines and data collection with triangulation to inform progress reporting on expected results in addition to integrating project performance qualitative measures looking at policy/enabling environment transformations.
73. The implementation timetable has been found to follow the required structure and format. The milestones and deliverables are well articulated and reporting timelines are provided as required.

## 4.6 Legal assessment

74. The Accreditation Master Agreement was signed with the Accredited Entity on 15 December 2016 (the 'AMA'), and it became effective on 20 February 2017.
75. The Accredited Entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.
76. The proposed project/programme will be implemented in Timor-Leste, a country in which GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities. This means that, amongst other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed. A draft agreement between GCF and Timor-Leste has been prepared and negotiations with the Government of Timor-Leste are ongoing, as of 20 August 2019.

<!-- image -->

77. The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have both expressed that it would not be legally feasible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where the GCF is not provided with relevant privileges and immunities. Therefore, it is recommended that disbursements by the GCF are made only after the GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country, or has been provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

## 4.7 List of proposed conditions (including legal)

78. In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that any approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:
2. (a) Signature of the funded activity agreement in a form and substance satisfactory to the GCF Secretariat within 180 days from the date of Board approval; and
3. (b) Completion of the legal due diligence to the satisfaction of the GCF Secretariat.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.30/02/Add.03

Page 132

## Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP171

| Proposal name:          | Enhancing early warning systems to build greater resilience to hydro-meteorological hazards in Timor-Leste   |
|-------------------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Accredited entity:      | United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP)                                                                  |
| Country/(ies):          | Democratic Republic of Timor-Leste                                                                           |
| Project/programme size: | Small                                                                                                        |

## I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

## 1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

1. Timor-Leste is a post-conflict newly independent country in the Asia-Pacific region. This small island developing State (SIDS) continues to be faced with hazards that are linked with climate variability and change, including tropical cyclones and occasional storm surge and subsequent inundation, extreme rainfall events, flooding, landslides in hilly terrains and prolonged droughts. The climate is generally tropical, which is influenced by intercontinental scale upper air circulation and El Niño/Southern Oscillation. Most of the population in the country are poor and their livelihoods and well-being depend on nature-based production systems. It is the latter reality that makes the majority of the population vulnerable to climatic perturbation and associated hazards. In addition to the livelihoods of the people, their food security, water supply and overall economic growth are largely dependent on managing climate-related hazards.
2. Globally available literature report on indications that the frequency and intensity of many known hazards in the South Pacific islands of the Asia-Pacific region will only increase under climate change. It is widely known that if advisory notices and fairly accurate early warnings are disseminated with adequate lead time, people who are engaged in primary production-based livelihoods and have little or no formal education can safeguard their livelihoods and even lives. There have been relentless efforts worldwide to develop computer programs that can generate forecasts with adequate lead times so that people can make their choices and responses and plan early, on the basis of such advisory notices and forecasts. With the advent of much better algorithms in computational models, the genesis and subsequent movement of life-threatening super cyclones and other weather fronts can be predicted and people under potential risk can be made aware of such impinging hazards.
3. These technological breakthroughs are available to only a handful of countries and, unfortunately, not many of them are developing countries. Those countries do not have skilled human resources to apply advanced multi-disciplinary science, or the computational software is too costly, or perhaps there is no effort towards continuously monitoring and gathering physical data in order to calibrate the models for local-level customization. In many developing countries the institutions having the national mandate to do all the above may not be duly supported with adequate annual budgetary allocations. Without an intent to simultaneously upgrade all aspects of an advanced climate information system, the overwhelming weaknesses cannot be addressed by ensuring international support for certain aspects of system development.

<!-- image -->

4. In addition, the legal meaning of 'climate information' may be missing, which is why such advanced climate information cannot be provided to the potential users. The usual meteorological description of temperature and rainfall amounts, which are often provided through official channels as climate information, are written in specialist terminology that prevents people from understanding the various dimensions of an impinging weather event. In many instances, dissemination protocols for climate information have not been written or implemented, which eventually becomes a barrier to disseminating warnings or advisory notices. Lack of finance for regular operation and maintenance (O&amp;M) is another ongoing problem in many developing countries. The institutions involved require legally binding protocols so that they can raise finance from the advisory users and contribute sufficient finance for the future sustainability of the early warning system (EWS).
5. Timor-Leste is a least developed country that has not yet dealt with the generation and subsequent dissemination of advanced climate information. Its current institutional capacity is at a rudimentary state, and is being supported with a very small budgetary allocation to employ only a handful of people. The development partners have provided financial support for equipment and technical assistance from time to time. However, because of a shortage of human resources and the absence of planning, the facilities and equipment are in a dilapidated condition and require immediate replacement. There are climate information services; however, such services are below the 'basic' category described in the international standard set out by the World Meteorological Organization (WMO).
6. The above discussion highlights why Timor-Leste needs a significant capacity-building effort so that the country can make its citizens aware of any climate-driven hazard. If this can be achieved, useful sector-specific advisory notices can be developed and disseminated so that people can integrate the information into their decision-making and planning frameworks. This capacity-building involves integration between the disaster risk reduction (DRR) sector and other sectors that cut across people's livelihoods and well-being. However, all of this cannot be achieved by Timor-Leste alone: it must achieve the advanced capacity through international collaboration with centres of excellence across the wider Asia-Pacific region and the world. Such collaboration is needed for aspects such as meteorology, oceanography, hydrology and hydraulics (pertaining to flooding and water resources engineering), to name a few. This should ideally be complemented with in-country collaboration and coordination among various institutions.
7. In view of the growing toll on Timor-Leste in terms of loss and damage resulting from the increasing impacts of climate change, it is necessary to provide adequate support to the country for upgrading the extremely weak climate information capacity. The funding proposal aims to deliver most of the necessary items for upgrading Timor-Leste's institutional capacity on climate information. United Nations Environment Programme (UNEP) is the accredited entity (AE) for the project. The Secretary of State of the Environment (SSE) is the executing entity for the project. SSE works closely with National Directorate for Meteorology and Geophysics (DNMG), and the latter is the primary driver of all deliverables under the project during the project period of five years and beyond. The AE, SSE and the DNMG will be supported by a host of reputable international bodies and institutions in various capacities.
8. The project has four components:
6. (a) Establishing institutional and policy frameworks for climate information and multihazard early warning services;
7. (b) Strengthening observation, monitoring, analysis and forecasting of climate and its impacts;
8. (c) Improving dissemination and communication of risk information and early warning; and

<!-- image -->

- (d) Enhancing climate risk management capacity.
9. A well-analysed baseline situation is one of the key determining factors that enables a clear understanding of the impact of any targeted intervention in a project. Following questions raised by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP), the proponents delivered a needs assessment study for climate information services in Timor-Leste. The study identified the national-level stakeholders, including their strengths and weaknesses. DNMG is the overall custodian for generating, collating and disseminating climate information in the country. While DNMG has the legal mandate to develop and deliver climate information, there are other national institutions that are integral to such information services. These institutions include the National Disaster Management Directorate, the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries, the National Directorate for Climate Change, the Ministry of Public Works and the National Directorate for Water Resources Management. In addition to these national organizations, there are other organizations and agencies that work closely with community members and are included as important stakeholders.
10. The needs assessment study states that the Government of Timor-Leste offers a category 1 level Basic Climate Service through the involvement of DNMG, as per the definitions provided by WMO. DNMG has recently enhanced its global-level collaboration with WMO in order to improve its efforts to deliver useful climate information. However, among many deficiencies, the highly inadequate capacity for modelling and projection makes the delivery of reliable and useful climate information extremely difficult.
11. Stakeholder consultations involving key actors and general stakeholders have found that there exists high demand for climate information with extended lead times involving both meteorological and hydrological products. In addition, there is a national legal mandate calling for climate-related early warnings and advisory notices to be provided. However, the legal mandate cannot currently be fulfilled because of organizational, human resources and financerelated barriers. Relevant policies are also in place, but national efforts to translate policies into actions are scanty and fragmented. There is a lack of adequate protocols for information dissemination in a form and substance that can be used effectively by the general population and all sectors, public and private. There is a great need to enhance national capacities so that people's aspirations regarding climate information and DRR may be adequately addressed. This is why a project is needed to establish a proper climate information service in Timor-Leste.
12. To produce impact-based forecasts, information on meteorology and oceanography needs to be integrated with ground information and modelling-based forecasts on hydrology and hydraulics. The initial funding proposal had little indication of its modalities to integrate information concerning hydrology and hydraulics. However, the AE explained to the independent TAP that it plans to establish complementarity with the GCF-financed project FP109 titled 'Safeguarding communities and their physical assets from climate-induced disasters in Timor-Leste'. Following a request by the independent TAP, the AE arranged a formal cooperation with the respective AE for FP109 so that there is no shortage of hydrological as well as hydraulics-related data to feed into the relevant efforts likely to be undertaken for the project. The AE confirmed that, for the planned collaboration, the effort would not require any additional finance. This official collaboration is likely to enhance performance of planned forecasting, especially for water-related hazards and disasters.
13. The project focuses on establishing an impact-based forecasting early warning system (IBF-EWS). However, the project initially offered a number of sub-activities, that lacked adequate justification. On the basis of the initial assessment of independent TAP and subsequent communications, the AE has now focused on a project dealing with climate information services, which need no further explanation on climate relevance of the project. Since there is little continuous longitudinal meteorological data in this war-torn country, TimorLeste needs support from GCF to establish a system for collecting data and generating user-

<!-- image -->

- friendly forecasts. This itself is a major driver towards seeking GCF financing, for which no additional climate justification is required.
14. The project aims to reach the entire population of the country (about 1.3 million) with its climate information services. About 80 per cent (i.e. 1.034 million) of the population is considered as direct beneficiaries of the project: 51 per cent of the direct beneficiaries are male, while the rest are female. If meaningful and socially worthwhile forecasts and advisory notices are developed and judiciously disseminated in languages that are socially widely accepted, and if simple dissemination modalities are applied, achieving such a high target number for direct beneficiaries is possible. However, the timeframe to organize the institutional set-up, human resources, technologies including modelling skills - all within five years following inception appears somewhat overwhelming, especially as the AE acknowledges the extremely low capacity of the DNMG to deliver all these under the aegis of the SSE, the executing entity of the project. A well-coordinated all-out effort will be needed, involving all parties, in order to achieve the objectives of the project within the stipulated time frame.
15. A total budget of USD 21.729 million is proposed, of which GCF contribution is sought amounting to USD 20.980 million as grant. Co-financing of USD 0.748 million is offered by the Government of Timor-Leste.
16. In view of the above discussions, the independent TAP is of the opinion that the overall impact potential of the project is high.

## 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: Medium to high

17. The current provisions for making people (including farming communities) and businesses aware of climate/weather-induced hazardous phenomena are at a rudimentary state in Timor-Leste. The strength of the institution in charge, DNMG, is poor in terms of its current human capacity. 1  the skills and capacity of the staff to handle advanced data acquisition equipment and information and computer technologies, and the extremely low level of capacity to utilize budgetary allocations. The annualized national funding committed to the climate information sector is very low, 2  which explains why the equipment purchased at different stages to upgrade the capacity for acquiring real-time weather data could not be kept in a functional condition for long. 3
18. An information needs assessment has been done via stakeholders' participation and by applying perceptions of different groups of people. In line with the current information system, the funding proposal is designed primarily to address current institutional gaps and weaknesses in terms of (a) equipment for building a database on climatology and partly on oceanography; (b) climate modelling; (c) institutional capacity-building in terms of initial budgetary provisioning, human capacity and skills enhancement, monitoring and data acquisition frameworks, regulatory frameworks including operational protocols; (d) capacity-building for sector-specific climate information provision; (e) collaboration and coordination (involving other provisions including hydrology and hydraulics) to establish multi-hazard forecasting and a data management platform; and (f) establishing a financial arrangement for achieving longterm sustainability of the system.
19. If all the above items are to be delivered, mostly through the overwhelming support of the international collaborating centres of excellence and potentially a GCF-financed project

1  DNMG has only 23 staff, as indicated in sub-section 6.1.3 of the feasibility study report.

2  The 2019 national allocation for DNMG was about USD 0.3 million (as presented in sub-section 6.1.3 of the feasibility study report).

3  This in turn has led to poor retention of actual instrumentation capacity on the ground, leading to data gaps.

<!-- image -->

- FP109, a paradigm shift may be achieved. Such a shift would ensure that a transition is made from an ill-informed reactive response approach, to an informed and planned response regime. The funding proposal indicates that the proposed project would achieve all the above and more within five years of its inception. However, the independent TAP considers the timeline to be extremely challenging.
20. The challenge regarding a short timeline can be overcome, especially when the bulk of the project actions will be accomplished by internationally established partners and partly by recruited international consultants. However, the intention for implementing capacity enhancement from a rudimentary level to deliver professional-level services by retaining newly acquired skills, equipment and human resources appears overwhelmingly difficult, if not impossible, during the five years duration of the project. More specifically, the weak capacity of the recipient institution (i.e. DNMG), even after strengthening, appears to pose a major challenge regarding its ability to sustain its functions following the departure of the large number of international consultants and the paid support of the institutions likely to collaborate with the project. Following feedback from the independent TAP the funding proposal was enhanced to include an exit strategy. The strategy indicates how DNMG will either draw or generate finance so that the mobilization of the newly recruited and subsequently trained specialists as well as the international specialists can be ensured following the cessation of the project support. The DNMG must execute the plan if the efforts of the project are to be sustainable.
21. The realization of paradigm shift through the project relies heavily on successful establishment of complementarity, where integration of early forecasting on aspects of hazards in relation to change in hydrology and hydraulics is likely to be achieved. However, the fate of such collaboration largely depends on the respective national actors. In the case of FP109, the ministry in charge is not one of the executing entities, nor it is included in the decision-making body for the project management. In the absence of all such avenues of direct engagement in the current funding proposal, the AE took the initiative and formally engaged with the AE for FP109 to sign a memorandum of understanding. Arrangements are being made to facilitate the process of data acquisition and continued integration regarding hydrology and hydraulics into the current IBF-EWS platform. Such collaboration is crucial for achieving paradigm shift.
22. The project is expected to provide early warning and climate information for about 80 per cent of the population, which means that there is hardly any room for replication within Timor-Leste. However, this project may inspire other regional SIDS to try to replicate the project. Although at the outset it appears that this replication potential is somewhat good for populations in aspiring countries, this also raises a red flag because, if the recipient institutions are not strong enough to run the models and integrate multi-disciplinary sciences and various state-of-the-art technologies, it might turn into an eventual failure. Instead, the independent TAP recommends a regional SIDS-wide approach towards developing an IBF-EWS, with adequate financing under a recipient body such as WMO, which might be more sustainable in the longer run.
23. The project intends to deal with fit-for-purpose equipment and technologies that are already in common use. The approach to deliver climate information services by capacitybuilding and upgrading the system is also common. Therefore, there is little scope for innovation as such. However, there is locally relevant innovation in terms of blending meteorological and hydrological data to generate people-centric DRR-related warnings and forecasts with extended lead time for people to react early. The design of best dissemination pathways must be carefully accomplished with innovation so that the use of multimedia and social media can be exploited to the fullest, keeping in view the actual penetration of such technologies in Timor-Leste.

<!-- image -->

24. The project is about capacity-building. Therefore, potential for knowledge and learning is at the centre of most activities. There will be training events for trainers to acquire up-to-date knowledge on the use of advanced technologies. Those train-the-trainer events will be supplemented by further localized training events. The stakeholders will be trained to understand advisory notices and integrate into DRR and local-level planning. The local institutions will be given training to integrate such lessons learned into their daily business, especially in dealing with DRR. The logical framework has presented tools to monitor and report on the progress of delivery.
25. The stipulated time frame for the project is too short for the creation of a market for climate information. Moreover, the AE notes that any future market-based demand on climate information services would necessitate demonstration of results, which may be accomplished within the stipulated five years. Real market expansion might happen after that.
26. There is no Standing Orders on Disaster (SOD) document. However, the project proponents aspire to create a climate information dissemination protocol that is legally bound. There is no placeholder in the proposal for conducting extensive advocacy at the national scale for the complete development of a legal instrument such as a SOD. The AE along with DNMG must inspire the Government of Timor-Leste to develop the SOD, otherwise the enactment of legislation on advisory notices and forecasts could be problematic. It is up to the Government to simultaneously ensure that both the SOD and legislation on advisory notices and forecasts have a legally binding regulatory framework. The policy environment is conducive to pushing this agenda. The project can push the agenda by involving the key stakeholders and coordinate such activities.
27. In view of the above discussions, the independent TAP finds the paradigm shift potential medium to high.

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

28. The project is expected to contribute towards achieving the sustainable development goals (SDGs) in a number of ways. The most direct contribution will be to SDG13 by means of taking actions to combat climate change and its impacts. In any given set-up, the development of multi-hazard EWS and dissemination of reliable forecasts help with safeguarding people's livelihoods and food security, occasionally save lives in the case of rapid occurring hazards, and significantly reduce the burdens of loss and damage.
29. Assuming smooth delivery of the project elements, the proposed project would contribute (indirectly) to the following SDGs:
3. (a) SDG 2 (zero hunger): helping farming communities by providing forecasts with increased lead time to enable them to address insecurities due to the occurrence of extreme weather events;
4. (b) SDG 3 (good health and well-being): giving people lead time to take precautionary measures towards safeguarding livelihoods and assets from being adversely affected by climatic hazards;
5. (c) SDG 5 (gender equality): allowing women to participate equally in life-saving activities as well as contributing in DRR-related local actions; and
6. (d) SDG 17 (partnership): helping the recipient country to forge a healthy partnership regime on generation and contribution to a globally acceptable climate dataset for the use of global communities.
30. Environmental co-benefits : The project proposal does not establish direct linkages between climate change and enhanced forest fire. The majority of fires in Timor-Leste, referred

<!-- image -->

- to by the AE as 'wild fires', are in fact generated by slash-and-burn agricultural practices that have been used for the past 10 years. However, a reliable forecast regarding rising temperatures beyond a given upper threshold could offer management options, thereby reducing chances of future occurrences of forest fires. This is how forest fire-related emissions can potentially but indirectly be reduced, while biodiversity in the forest could also benefit.
31. Economic co-benefits : The project offers potential economic co-benefits. First, the farming communities are likely to benefit from receiving and following weather advisory notices and planning their crop rotations early, thereby safeguarding the standing crops from imminent climatic hazards. The Government can take necessary measures against potential destruction of infrastructure in case of a cyclonic storm surge or a potential landslide. The intended change in the regulatory regime might create opportunities to take coordinated steps towards reducing the burden of loss and damage, especially in view of high-impact events.
32. Although it appears too optimistic that the implementation of forecast-based financing (FbF) could be delivered on time (i.e. within the stipulated five years of project implementation), once the background works have been accomplished, the business sector is likely to receive information suitable for its needs (which need to be established first), and the active participation of the business sector will pave the way for increased sustainability in the long run. The project may not deliver an effective FbF regime, but it may pave the way for a future FbF regime for the country.
33. Social co-benefits : The proposed EWS is likely to accrue social benefits. A life saved from drowning in an open sea as a result of the dissemination of an early warning has huge social implications. Similarly, early detection of drought in the face of a change in the behaviour of the El Niño/Southern Oscillation in the Pacific Ocean can safeguard production by thousands of farmers, by giving them adequate time to protect their standing crops. One such advisory notice can avoid the hunger of millions of citizens. The social co-benefits likely to be accrued through the intended integration of DRR can be significant. However, such efforts need integration of data and information on hydrology and hydraulics, in addition to climatology and oceanography. The collaboration with the executive entity of FP109 and other allied organizations will be of great help in achieving such co-benefits.
34. The modelling-based forecasts can be quite useful in generating health advisory notices regarding projections on heat stress, especially in case of urban heat stress. When advisory notices are generated and disseminated, the health-care system can find ways to manage health risks, particularly of young children and older people.
35. The proposed project is likely to integrate gender concerns in relation to accessing climate information and taking necessary response measures. If the stakeholder engagement is designed in a gender-friendly manner, it is likely that women will have equal access to technology-driven early warnings. However, utmost care is necessary to design the last-mile dissemination strategy. If gender-friendly modalities of accessing climate advisory notices are not considered in the design and the actual dissemination occurs at times and in places where women are unlikely to receive the information, the ultimate objectives around gender equality will not be achieved.
36. The proposal intends to arrange a gender awareness programme and also proposes measures to address gender-differentiated vulnerabilities through meaningful stakeholder engagements. Moreover, the project intends to integrate gender concerns by incorporating equal representation of women and equal participation in planning, decision-making, capacitybuilding and skills enhancement programmes. Extreme care is needed towards implementation of the gender action plan for the project.
37. In view of the above considerations, the independent TAP is of the opinion that the sustainable development potential of the project is high.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.30/02/Add.03

Page 139

## 1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

38. Timor-Leste is not only a SIDS, it is also one of the least developed countries. Over 70 per cent of its population are currently facing poverty. Widespread deprivation also exists on a number of aspects such as health care, education and standard of living. Malnutrition is also widespread, being manifest in over half of the children, while most women and girls are anaemic. Against such a backdrop, the country is ranked 131 out of 189 on the human development index.
39. The country has faced a war of independence that lasted for about a quarter of a century from 1975 to 1999. The war devastated critical infrastructure and killed one third of the population. By the time the country began to rebuild, climate variability and change had begun to contribute additional stresses in the form of extreme weather events. The country has experienced a general rise in surface temperature and a seasonal decline in rainfall - which generally occurs in the most critical periods for crop growth. As a consequence, crop agriculture is facing challenges of loss of surface moisture during critical crop growing periods. Moreover, heat stress has been causing health-related problems that affect people's well-being. The denudation during the war of independence and erratic extreme rainfall events are thought to be causing landslides in undulated terrains, further causing degradation of forest ecosystems and their biodiversity. All such factors have culminated into the overall high vulnerability of Timor-Leste, ranking it fifteenth in a list of countries in the world most at risk of natural disaster, according to World Risk Index 2019.
40. Agriculture is a critically important sector in the country, providing a subsistence living for over 80 per cent of the country's population. Slash-and-burn agricultural practices are rampant in the hilly terrains, often causing forest fires. Change in rainfall patterns is claimed to be affecting crop agriculture and thereby people's food security. Flooding due to incessant rainfall events is common, while extreme rainfall events trigger landslides. These extreme events are believed to be adversely affecting the country's development by destroying both public and private infrastructure in a war-torn country, and disrupting various essential services, including transport and health.
41. Under these circumstances, the country needs to generate and disseminate early warnings with increased lead time so that citizens can plan and execute adaptive responses. However, the infrastructure has been partly destroyed, disrupting the collection of data - a critical input towards running forecast models. There have been numerous efforts in the past by development partners to help the country to partially rebuild the war-ravaged weather infrastructure. However, due to lack of regular maintenance, the equipment has become inoperable, resulting in fragmentation of the data sets available for better understanding of the climate system. The lack of O&amp;M can be partly explained by severe lack of investment in O&amp;M, which is evident by the very low level of deployment of human resources for the job and an overall minuscule budgetary allocation for the handful of staff maintained by DNMG.
42. In order for Timor-Leste to make headway in advanced climate information provisioning, the country and its relevant institutions need international support. First of all, the dilapidated and outdated instruments for weather and oceanographic data collection need to be replaced by advanced technologies. The institution in charge, DNMG, must redesign its human resources and budgetary allocations so that the staff can embrace digital challenges in handling data and generating computer model-based climate information. Therefore, the DNMG must elevate its standing through a massive capacity-building effort, following a logical step-wise process, so that the institution can take full charge of generating and subsequently disseminating reliable climate information services.
43. However, given the annual level of public spending, it is beyond the current capacity of the Government of Timor-Leste to bear the full cost of maintaining a fully functional EWS. While

<!-- image -->

Page 140

- the Government is keen on doing something meaningful in this regard, its lack of ability to provide finance is undeniable. The Government cannot invest heavily in climate information services while a large majority of the population is facing abject poverty. This is why international support is needed. The need for a GCF grant is well understood.
44. Lack of financing is certainly a barrier in the process. However, it is the near nonexisting capacity of the DNMG which is the largest barrier. There is no question whether the institution needs support to build its capacity to deliver at a basic level: the critical question is how far the institution can organize itself to absorb the added capacity within the stipulated time frame of five years. The DNMG must approach the capacity-building issue in a systematic manner, perhaps one little step at a time so that the newly recruited staff and technologists do not succumb to the pressure of delivering high-quality climate information products within the project lifetime. The international collaboration proposed under the project needs to be extended for a few more years following project completion. If necessary, the DNMG needs to arrange government and/or international financing to ensure the continuation of international support from centres of excellence until the local trained actors are fully capable of running the programme on their own.
45. Since the country is prone to climatic hazards, integration of hydrology and hydraulics with meteorology and oceanography is extremely important, especially if the climate information is expected to assist the DRR sector. The integration of the DRR sector cannot be fully realized if the Government does not streamline its allied regulatory framework. There is a need to develop and enact a SOD, which will pave the way to promulgate laws in relation to dissemination of disaster-related forecasts and advisory notices. The lack of an appropriate legal regime is also understood as a current barrier. Efforts must be made by the project to address the barrier and forge a mechanism to help the Government deliver the legal and regulatory regime.
46. Fortunately, the current barriers have been identified in the feasibility study report of the proposal and addressed in the proposal. However, questions remain as to whether all such barriers can be removed within the stipulated five years, since the journey will have to be started from scratch and there is hardly any prior experience of dealing with advanced technologies and equipment to deliver EWS in Timor-Leste.
47. The independent TAP finds the needs of the recipient to be high.

## 1.5 Country ownership Scale: Medium to high

48. The alignment of the proposed project with national policy documents is commendable. A number of policy documents have highlighted the need for an advanced set-up for the generation and dissemination of climate information services. The policy alignment is found in the following documents: (a) national adaptation plan of action (2010); (b) national disaster risk management policy (2018); (c) initial national communication to the United Nations Framework Convention on Climate Change (2014); and (d) the nationally determined contribution (2016). The project is also included in the GCF Country Programme 2019, which indicates a sense of utter necessity expressed by the recipient country. The objectives of the project are mostly in line with priorities in the above-mentioned policy documents.
49. UNEP is the AE for the project. UNEP also has the additional role of being an executing entity, perhaps a natural inclusion in view of the extremely poor capacity of both the other executing entity (i.e. SSE) and the principal support recipient organization, DNMG. However, the AE has also included a large number of allied international organizations to help bridge the current capacity gaps. The team as a whole can deliver the project in any given country within the Asia-Pacific region.

<!-- image -->

50. The institutional arrangements compared with the need for the multi-faceted nature of delivering IBF-EWS (i.e. involving a large number of national and international actors) appears challenging to deliver the objective of the project. The project is overwhelmingly dependent on external institutions and actors (i.e. consultants). The exit strategy explains how the newly recruited human resources will be kept working, involving budgetary allocations from the Government. There is a plan to gather funds towards meeting the costs of O&amp;M that will be procured under the project.
51. It has been observed throughout the world that it becomes extremely challenging to retain skilled modellers (across the disciplines such as meteorology, hydrology and oceanography) in developing countries due to mismatch of compensation packages in-country and outside. The exit strategy indicates the importance of arranging adequate funds for the retention of newly recruited locally available human resources following extensive skills enhancement through intensive training. It is anticipated that Timor-Leste will ensure sustainability of its human resources through institutional arrangements as well as budgetary provisioning.
52. In the view of the independent TAP, integration with globally reputable centres of excellence is subject to many factors, including subcontracting. Moreover, the critical aspect of integration of hydrology and hydraulics is likely to be via the national executing entity of FP109. Fortunately, the institutional arrangements are adequately made so that continued collaborations may be envisaged.
53. The project has been developed through a consultative process. The project proposal states that such consultations have engaged civil society organizations that are involved in DRR and humanitarian activities, organizations working for the disable persons and so on. However, it is unclear whether or not representation by indigenous people and gender groups have been considered in the consultations. The national designated authority has been involved in the development of the project.
54. Based on the above-mentioned realities, the independent TAP rates country ownership as medium to high.

## 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

55. As indicated in paragraph 15 above, the Government of Timor-Leste has offered a total amount of USD 748,400.00 as co-financing, which constitutes 3.44 per cent of the total budget. The bulk of the financing is sought from GCF, which amounts to USD 20.980 million. The AE requests GCF to provide access to the amount with full concessionality (i.e. as grant).
56. While the financing part is justified by each sub-component, the independent TAP cannot ignore that a large amount is also going to keep national and international consultants who would offer capacity-building and other technical services. In this regard, the budgetary allocation is not among the most efficient expected by the GCF standard, particularly for projects aiming at achieving climate information services.
57. The AE states that the net present value (NPV) is USD 56.01 million and the economic internal rate of return (EIRR) is 104.3 per cent. The AE assumes a discount rate of 10 per cent. The positive NPV and the high EIRR indicate that the project is likely to be economically viable. The economic analysis has been extended to check the sensitivity of economic viability of the project. It appears that, if the benefits were reduced by 10 per cent, the resulting EIRR would be 90.2 per cent. Likewise, if the benefit streams are perturbed by a 10 per cent increase in project costs, the subsequent EIRR becomes 91.5 per cent. In both such problematic cases, the resulting EIRR falls within the 10 per cent threshold, and therefore still exhibits the high economic feasibility of the project.

<!-- image -->

GCF/B.30/02/Add.03 Page 142

58. A further sensitivity analysis assumed a combination of increasing costs and declining benefit streams by an equal 10 per cent, which results in a revised NPV of USD 46.81 million and a revised EIRR of 79.4 per cent. Both these number indicate high economic returns even if all the uncertainties are taken into consideration. The benefit-to-cost ratio of 5.7:1 indicates that for every USD 10 invested in the project, a likely return will be in the order of USD 57, which again indicates an economically highly desirable project.
59. The project intends to build on existing networks and strengths in the country. However, the funding proposal illustrates that the current state of affairs of the network is rather poor, and needs to be overhauled with new equipment and regular O&amp;M. Since new financing will be dedicated to procuring weather data collecting equipment, and future O&amp;M financing is assured, it is most likely that the data generating network will sustain and continue to deliver.
60. Initially, the project rightly focused on the equipment for data collection to support analyses of climatology and oceanography. However, following revisions the funding proposal indicated the intention to integrate data from other projects (i.e. FP109) and continue to gather data from other national institutions. The necessary institutional agreement has been forged, which gives general assurance that an inter-institutional collaboration will be ensured by the project.
61. The funding proposal is optimistic in terms of accommodating diversity and the volume of work within the stipulated timeframe. The inclusion of Component 4 is perhaps a demonstration of such optimism. Since Timor-Leste is starting almost from scratch on providing climate information, the five years appear just about adequate to deliver the IBF-EWS. Since the executing agency has to build capacity to integrate aspects related to information on hydrology and hydraulics, the inclusion of Component 4 appears useful in the longer term. However, it would add challenges regarding overall implementation.
62. From the above analysis, the independent TAP is of the opinion that project efficiency and effectiveness is medium to high.

## II. Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

63. On the basis of the above findings, the independent TAP recommends that the Board approve the project.
